in writing. the european parliament's report is in line with the commission's proposal for a regulation on public access to special substances for general use, which can however be used as precursors to explosives. the union is called upon to protect the free movement of goods and to ensure the safety of citizens against the possible illegal use of these substances. this regulation, which i voted in favour of, clearly regulates the concentration levels which render these substances inactivated for the production of household explosives and precisely defines the legal framework for their circulation and use. in any event, the implementation and effectiveness of such measures will depend on the member states and the level of cooperation they develop in this field.
in writing. − i voted in favour of this proposal because it restricts access by members of the public to specific substances in general use but which can also be misused as explosives precursors. furthermore in protecting the free movement of goods, the proposal suggests that eight substances listed in annex i may continue to be sold in concentrated form under a licence granted by a national competent authority for a documented legitimate purpose or, without a licence, at concentration levels which render them ineffective for the manufacture of home-made explosives.
in writing. i voted in favour of the report on the eu's accession to the overseas protocol to the barcelona convention, which protects the mediterranean from pollution by offshore exploration and exploitation of its seabed. although it is not possible to eliminate the risks of such mining entirely, the 'offshore protocol' is a tool that provides safety standards for the protection of the marine environment, environmental impact assessment and environmental responsibility. of course, much stricter safety standards would be needed in the mediterranean because it is a particularly vulnerable area due to its semi-enclosed nature and significant seismicity and because exploration and exploitation activities in the area are constantly increasing. for its inhabitants, the mediterranean plays a vital role in their daily lives and i believe that, beyond the environmental dimension, the ratification of the protocol by the eu will allow us to work in harmony with our non-eu mediterranean partners to ensure better protection of this sea for all its users. the fact that mediterranean states with bad relations are cooperating under the barcelona convention and its protocols demonstrates the extent to which environmental protection can contribute to peace and international cooperation.
in writing. the european parliament's report, which i voted in favour of, is a consequence of the well-known 'barcelona convention', signed in 1976 and amended in 1995. under this, the european union and all eu mediterranean coastal member states comply with basic principles for the protection of the mediterranean (licensing, removal of abandoned or decommissioned installations and harmful substances) and take responsibility for the marine environment. coastal activities in the mediterranean may increase due to the discovery of new fossil fuel reserves, both in cyprus and in greece, and therefore offshore oil and gas extraction activities should be managed in such a way as to prevent an accident with direct large-scale effects in a semi-enclosed sea such as the mediterranean. greece is therefore committed to this and the measures already taken to exploit its wealth are in line with the agreements it has signed.
in writing. − i voted in favour of this report because the consumer credit directive has two objectives: guaranteeing a high level of consumer protection and strengthening the internal market in cross-border consumer credit. there is no doubt that the definitive harmonisation of some key aspects of credit law has significantly raised the level of consumer protection in europe.
in writing. i voted in favour of this report. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this is a serious mistake. social investment is investment against poverty, against exclusion from opportunity, in favour of skills, qualifications and humanity. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the excellent work he has done. the inactive fellow human being and therefore the unused cell of our society is transformed into a healthy, productive and efficient one. social investment is therefore an investment in people, in social cohesion. as such they should be regarded, especially in these critical times, when the crisis in europe is not only economic but also, in many cases, social and values-based.
in writing. − i voted in favour of this report which clearly identifies that the union’s promotion policy should reward farmers and food promotion actions where substantial efforts have been made to implement production systems which respond to the new challenges european farmers face. for this reason, the budget for the horizontal scheme, which currently represents less than 0.1 % of cap spending, should be increased.
in writing. online shopping is experiencing a steady and significant growth across europe. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's financial interests are protected. taking into account the specificities of online transactions, any regulation at community level should be made gradually and collectively by the member states. one step in this direction is better integration of online, or mobile, markets that are still fragmented in the eu today. the process of establishing a single euro payments area (sepa) facilitates bank transfers in 32 european countries and by 2014 all participating countries should have implemented the common sepa standards. the report, which i voted in favour of, recommends effective ways of operating the ems and also suggests the next steps to be taken towards a single european framework for european trade.
in writing. the rapid introduction of new technologies often creates difficulties for parents and teachers in being able to communicate them effectively and positively to children. the resulting gap provides a platform for cyber threats of all kinds, making it necessary to protect children in the digital world. this own-initiative report provides for action both at the level of national education systems and in terms of specific conditions for providers of online services. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. the effective protection of children also lies to a large extent in the family. however, it is clear that the protection of children should in no way be equated with exclusion from new technologies, which are now an essential resource for increasing children's qualifications and skills. in the same spirit, protection should be combined with increased investment in new technologies, particularly in countries facing budgetary problems. the report, which i voted in favour of, covers this aspect satisfactorily.
in writing. i voted in favour of mrs costa's report because i believe it contributes to the harmonisation of efforts in the field of the protection of minors in the digital world. there is no doubt that the internet offers many opportunities and new tools for communication, education and training for children who are of exceptional importance. however, as the report itself stresses, it is important to ensure the quality standards that content aimed at minors must meet and to commit member states to protecting minors in the digital world.
in writing. − i voted in favour of this report because social enterprises are important drivers of sustainable development and social innovation and they have a major role to play in achieving the social, employment and educational targets of the eu 2020 strategy. a favourable climate for social enterprises in combination with a strong political framework and the necessary funding will allow for these enterprises to survive in the current crisis and to thrive.
in writing. the kke voted against the report which is moving in the same reactionary and anti-people direction as the van roobey plan. the plans of the political staff of the monopolies for the economic and fiscal integration of the eu - the future structure, form and functioning of the eurozone and the eu - are made from the perspective of serving the interests of capital. at the same time, they are reinforcing the decentralising tendencies and the acute rivalries, both within the eu and with the other imperialist powers, over who will benefit from the capitalist crisis at the expense of the other. the solutions to the crisis proposed by bourgeois governments and the eu are determined by the criterion of strengthening the profitability of the monopolies, through a savage attack on the lifestyles and rights of workers. this road is exacerbating the problems of the people and preparing the way for the next crisis, much sooner than the political representatives of capital expect. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. a people-friendly way out of the crisis presupposes disengagement from the eu, unilateral debt cancellation for over-indebted capitalist states and socialization of monopolies with people power - economy.

rapporteur. − madam president, europe has the longest history in the world of the pursuit of progress and democracy. it was because of those european men and women who boldly and bravely paved the way for the exploration and exploitation of the continent’s resources that europe became one of the leading powers in politics and economy. shale gas gives us all the opportunity to prove that our generation of politicians considers the continent’s natural resources and wealth as a challenge for growth and not as a threat. the recent evolution in the european context of shale gas developments suggests a growing need for a clear, predictable and coherent approach to unconventional fossil fuels. this is how we reach optimal decisions in an area involving social and environmental issues, industrial competitiveness and, in particular, public trust. based on american experience – because we do not have any european experience on this – shale gas is potentially the biggest energy development since the 1920s, as big a change as when we switched from using coal to oil. the shale gas-related developments in the usa will have a profound impact on global energy demand and geopolitics. the us has already surpassed russia as the world’s largest gas producer and the price of gas in the us has collapsed, making the price in the us between usd 3 and 4 per unit, while in europe the price is usd 10-14. a european single market for gas has been a concept we have been hearing about from the commission for some time now. one of the key and legitimate concerns of the european energy liberalisation project was that liberalisation would have limited effect unless there were new sources of gas. if states were still largely dependent on the same external suppliers for their gas liberalisation, a single market would not prosper. shale gas could add a new dynamic to the european gas market. this in turn could lead to increasing global gas to gas competition, having a major effect on prices and avoiding indexation to oil prices. with a correct safety approach, highest safety standards and the application of best available technologies, shale gas could be a huge gain to eu energy security and diversity, as well as a potential contributing factor to europe’s reindustrialisation in the years to come. europe needs a vision and a clear message of how gas fits into its energy policy. whether we use shale gas or not, in as little time as we have been hearing about it we have already been witnesses to its significance as a global energy trendsetter for markets, energy demand and cost partners. shale gas is perhaps not the panacea for europe’s energy problems overall. unconventional fuels are still at an infant stage in europe and we should explore the options available and the possible benefits as rigorously as we examine and analyse the potential environmental effects. on the commission’s side, coordination between dg energy, dg enterprise, dg environment and dg climate action will now be vital as you take this forward. commissioners, i wish you good luck for the sake, the goodwill and the survival of european citizens.
colleagues, i just wanted to inform you that as regards blue cards i will accept those cards which are raised during the initial speech of the speaker and not during the question which is put to him. apparently we will have a lot of blue cards during this discussion.
mr president, this is a general question to everybody who has spoken up to now. they referred to lobbyists. please, since i am the rapporteur of the committee on industry, research and energy (itre), i do not need any lobbyists to influence me. i am old enough to have my own opinion. so, with all due respect, please, criticise what i have written but do not refer to my text as being the outcome of lobbyists or influenced by lobbyists. this is my text. the shadow rapporteurs have worked on this and we have come to a consensus on many things. this is the product of collective work that has been done within the itre committee. the criticism should be of us as writers with no reference to lobbying or that lobbyists have influenced us. the second question is the following. since i am the rapporteur on this, do you not think that i have taken into account all the hazards and all the risks that shale gas may pose? as i told you from the beginning, this is a challenge for growth and not a threat. this is the scope in which i have seen this text.
mr president, we are discussing rules and standards for this particular type of mining, but we must not forget that these rules and standards must also be respected. i would like to say, as other colleagues have done, that i really do condemn the trade in hope that has been built up around these potential reserves. we are talking specifically about natural gas reserves. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. if we knew where and how much of these reserves are, we wouldn't need to do any research. but it seems that some people want to deceive the growing number of unemployed in our countries and to mislead public opinion by offering not real solutions to unemployment but hopes of a future paradise. i ask that this demagogy and this trade in hope be stopped here and now. we must tell the truth to our fellow citizens. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the gas prices are not lower than they should be. the price of oil is decided on the international stock exchanges. natural gas is linked to this price. the prices of both are rising steadily. real energy independence is to get rid of coal.
member of the commission. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, the commission has already made a number of proposals. we are grateful for the many suggestions, criticisms and questions and will certainly continue our work between several commissioners and our services on this basis in the coming weeks. it's about precision over speed. the rules of procedure have been laid down today. what we do not have is a mandate from the energy policy side or a general mandate to decide on shale gas with a yes or no. a ban or a general authorization without further commitments cannot be the competence of european policy. what we have, what my colleague has first of all, are the rules in force, a valid european legal framework for environmental protection, for soil and groundwater protection and also for health protection. it must be respected. member states are obliged to comply. if there's any wrongdoing, you investigate it. the question is - and we are analysing this - whether we need to extend our legislation on the environment, climate, nature and health. secondly, the european energy mix is already being affected by the development of shale gas in america. we have today greater quantities of lng from different producing countries in the world in europe because this gas is no longer exported to the usa. this means that the americans are securing their own gas needs through shale gas, and we have access to lng gas with existing and planned lng terminals. the competition between member states to build new lng terminals and to receive support, whether through the eib or through the budget, is as great in the three baltic states as it is in the mediterranean, the adriatic, croatia, slovenia and italy. we have a second indirect advantage from shale gas in the united states. the russians were prepared to enter into negotiations, to go to arbitration with us, when it came to long-term contracts and maximum prices for gas, thanks to the shale gas revolution. and some of the gas contracts with the russians have already been changed and the gas price has been reduced somewhat because shale gas has brought a change, a discount on gas on the world market and will continue to bring. the order is therefore clear: we are reviewing the rules in force and are happy to update and extend them if necessary. one question i found intriguing: do we even need gas? i say to you explicitly that the energy mix, the technology, is a matter for the member states and i do not believe that a change to article 194 will be accepted by the member states in our generation because the circumstances of the member states are so different that the decision for or against nuclear power, or how long nuclear power, how much, for or against coal, gas, renewable energies is not left to the european level by the 28 member states. i don't have that expectation any time soon. if so, it is clear that important decisions are taken by the member states. i am in favour of a strong expansion of renewable energy. but 100 per cent renewable energy for transport, for heat, for electricity, for industry is a long-term vision at the moment, and if you want to achieve it too soon, it's an illusion. in fact, it was widely understood here in parliament that gas is the most flexible and environmentally friendly complementary energy source for security of supply alongside rising renewables. mr president, i would like to say to you that i am very pleased that the commission has taken the initiative of introducing a new system of energy prices for the community, which is a system which is not only a system of energy prices but also a system of energy prices for the community. that means you have to ask the question. i argue that we will need gas, whether conventional or non-conventional, for the next 30 years if the deployment of non-baseload, non-permanently available renewable energy sources such as solar and wind in europe is to remain compatible with security of supply and affordability. we cannot do without gas for the next few decades, and therefore ultimately - if we want to serve the co2 issue - we cannot do without ccs either. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in the three 20-20-20 criteria! they were created in 2007 by broad consensus, with the agreement of all member states in the european council and, at the same time, by a large majority in the european parliament. i have now started the discussion on new targets for 2030, because 2020 is tomorrow. and for investors, 2020 was yesterday. my request is this: despite all the disagreements, if we want to reach an agreement among all the member states, from belgium to germany to poland and the uk, on energy and climate policy and thus retain competence and authority in energy policy matters, also for the next and the next european parliament, then we need a willingness to compromise. if everyone here is trying to achieve their own ideals or to be a bigot, it's going to go wrong! we need a compromise between energy strategy and environmental protection. we need a willingness to compromise between countries that have 90% coal - poland today - and do not want to import gas from russia, because this is their lesson from their history, which is why poland is relying on nuclear power and on shale gas. we need the british to be willing to compromise so that, like tony blair, they are prepared to continue to europeanise energy and climate policy and not allow re-national developments to follow. because one thing is clear: when it comes to climate protection, each member state alone is of no global relevance! the eu-27 we are still responsible for 11% of global co2 emissions. if we do not stand together, if we do not compromise, if we do not condemn lobbying and dependence on lobbyists, if we do not set new pragmatic targets for 2030 on co2 emissions reductions, efficiency and renewable energy, then this success story of european energy and climate policy will end sooner than some people think, but with it our importance to washington and beijing and to others and our role as a model. so, apart from the issue of shale gas, i would ask you all to be willing to compromise if the success story of a european energy and climate policy is to continue with you or your successors in the next decade. i see difficulties at the moment, with the anti-european sentiment in a growing number of member states, with the incredible diversity in the energy mix and with the growing mistrust that we have seen in this house today, in terms of advancing binding targets on co2 emissions reductions, renewable energy and efficiency.
member of the commission. − mr president, honourable members, it is good that we have this debate in the afternoon rather than in the evening. i will try to start with some facts. there is still a lot of uncertainty when it comes to shale gas. reserves, best technology, influence on health, influence on the environment. i think the best proof for that fact is the differences in the positions in the member states; obviously they are basing their positions on something which is a solid analysis and they are coming to quite different conclusions. the second important fact is that it is true that the us is ahead of us. it is also true that this has changed their energy market there, but maybe in this case their being ahead of us is not so bad and we should learn from their experiences. by the way, they are also learning from their own experiences. if there is one thing that really is clear today, it is that we need more knowledge, we need more understanding, we need better studies and we should continue doing that. what we already know is that there are considerable risks in a number of areas: surface and ground water, air quality, land use, biodiversity, seismic risks, local communities’ traffic and noise disturbances. these have been identified as areas to which attention needs to be paid. you probably know that in september we published three studies. one study, on the economic potential, was made by günther oettinger’s services. another study, on climate impact, was carried out by connie hedegaard’s departments, and the third was on the environmental impact. what can be concluded – even if one conclusion is of course that we should continue with work of this kind in an analytical way – is that there are two conclusions we can draw from an environmental point of view and our analyses.first, the risks related to unconventional gas exploration are higher than in conventional gas exploration. second, existing environmental legislation on the ea, the ca, the mining directive, the water framework directive, reach and so on, is important and applicable and should be applied today. but it is not sufficient to address all the risks identified. so it is on that basis actually that we have proposed what we are here today together to explain. we have drawn up the proposal jointly. the form has not yet been decided because this is also connected, of course, with the impact assessment which we are starting, but i would just like to remind everybody that the international energy agency (iea) position also is, and i quote, ‘there is a clear need for a strict regulatory framework’. i would also like to touch on the question many of you brought up, the question of competitiveness. i am not denying, rather i am acknowledging, that some industries, especially industries which have higher energy inputs and costs of course, are very affected by how energy prices are moving, as are households. of course this is important. but to give you just one fact: last week i was at the vdi, at the german innovation engineering federation, and they have actually said quite clearly that the average input costs for german manufacturing industry are 2% energy, 42% materials and 18% labour. i think that this is why i am also paying so much attention, if you remember, to resource efficiency because i firmly believe this is one of the ways of not only addressing environmental concerns, but also of addressing issues connected to the competitiveness of europe. if we look at the trends in prices for resources, not just energy resources, but also energy resources, since the beginning of this century, we see that these prices have begun to go up fast and the expectation is that this will continue. so i think we should really focus and prepare regarding the questions of how we can improve resource productivity, resource efficiency and how we can also improve energy efficiency. the third issue i would like to mention from the competitiveness angle is that we can, of course, also learn the lesson that not respecting safety, human health and environmental health considerations can also be very costly for companies. we only need to recall the judgment last week imposing a usd 4.5 billion fine on british petroleum. we should really do everything possible to ensure something like that does not happen, that we manage the risks in an appropriate way. concerning the possibility of a ban: i think many of you mentioned that. it is more theoretical than actually practical because at the end of the day, you know the responsibilities of the member states under the treaty and we also know that if there were to be a proposal of this kind, we would need unanimity from the member states. in the context of the moratorium which many of you mentioned, what i would like to say is that activities in europe are currently at exploration stage. intensified drilling and hydraulic fracturing are not expected for another two to three years from now, but full-scale hydraulic fracturing will also be deployed during the exploration stage for pilot product testing and this may give rise to certain risks and very likely also to public concerns. the commission’s timeline for conducting the impact assessment is deemed suitable for identifying the most appropriate risk management measures, both with regard to exploration and to extraction. but i would like to emphasise that parliament should rest assured that the commission will keep abreast of and react to any development which would change that timeline. in this exercise it will be also important of course for member states to apply all the obligations pursuant to current union legislation. so where are we now? we have impact assessments in the making. the public consultation will be open until the end of the year and next year we plan to come with a risk management proposal which should efficiently and effectively address the existing risks. i think this is basically in everybody’s interests. clear, predictable, safe conditions under which exploitation could be possible, proper management of risks connected with human health and protection of the environment. we know that we have to address all those questions now. that is why we are rushing with our preparations and we shall do our best to earn the public trust needed. honestly, we have no guarantee, but we really need that trust. first and foremost, trust may be needed by the companies which could potentially invest huge amounts of money. finally, i think that shale gas has potential, especially as a substitute for coal. but we should not forget that shale gas is a fossil fuel and in that context we should not forget either that we are supporting the development of renewable energy with good reason, and we should continue doing that, as günther oettinger also mentioned. so finally i would like to thank you for the reports which were really timely and also for today’s quite lively discussion. rest assured that we will take your messages into consideration and the risks which you raised will be dealt with in our 2013 proposal. we will also consider risk management and regulatory shortcomings in our impact assessment.
rapporteur. − mr president, i would like to thank everybody for the very lively debate. i can see mr bütikofer smiling: you were indeed full of passion today. i thank the shadow rapporteurs from every political group. we cooperated very well – including mr bütikofer – and i think we came up with a good compromise text, even if it does come from the committee on industry, research and energy. the focus should be – and it is – on industry and energy. we did not ignore all the precautions that have to be taken by the industry when they are fracking. so i appeal to my colleagues for tomorrow’s vote. we have to recognise the potential in shale gas. it is not the solution for the future but it is a potential one for the transitional period. we have to compromise and see what the best alternative is that we have for meeting our targets for clean energy in europe. last and not least, from our side as politicians, we have to have a balance with the economic crisis that exists within the 27 member states. we have to respect the natural resources that each member state has and see that they are explored and exploited in the best way, using the best environmentally friendly methods and techniques. for the commissioners, it looks like we are at the beginning of a long journey concerning and referring to shale gas. we still have a long way to go and we still have to analyse all the impacts this will have, not only on the environment, but also on the competitiveness of the european economy. i think we have started well with this debate today and i wish good luck to this new, natural resource in europe. europe deserves it and we politicians have to make the best out of it in the best environmentally friendly way.
member of the commission. − mr president, let me stress again that i consider the situation at the ford factory in genk to be extremely serious. that is why eight days ago i received a delegation of the trade unions of this company and discussed with representatives of the employees themselves what the commission can do in this particular situation, particularly by using the european globalisation adjustment fund, but also in the broader context in cooperation with the authorities of belgium, also using the european social fund if allocations are available. but i also want to stress that similarly to many who have spoken in this debate, i consider the question of the industry to be extremely serious and very relevant. that is why, together with mr tajani, we have been working on a reinforced industrial policy for the european union which sets out a very concrete numerical objective until 2020: to reverse the industrial decline of europe, to increase the share of industry within the european economy to at least 20 % of the european gdp from its current level of 16%. of course this requires a very concerted effort. it also requires a sectoral view when necessary and when justified and clearly the automotive sector is one of those where we need a concrete sectoral focus and an action plan. in fact the cars 2020 action plan delivers. it delivers many requirements which you mentioned in this discussion. for example, in terms of innovation, it does not lower but actually increases the ambition. it also points to the possible financing tools by which the european union can help the revival of this sector. but, as mr harbour also pointed out, there is a need for a certain level of realism, a need to see what globalisation, global competition, cost-effectiveness means in this sector, what skills are needed in the future; and that is why the anticipation of change, the anticipation of structural development in cooperation with the social partners involved is so important in this area. this is actually one of the key lessons of our consultation, on the basis of the commission green paper, on restructuring which we conducted in the first half of this year; and on the basis of this consultation, which included a broader range of stakeholders and not only the social partners, we are very carefully considering the necessary steps forward. in that of course we pay very close attention to the work of mr cercas as rapporteur and certainly we will consider his conclusions, which i believe are forthcoming in december in this parliament, when we decide in the commission on the necessary steps forward. i certainly take note of many points made in this debate concerning the wish for a more robust, more interventionist industrial policy. i have to admit that what is there, a 2.0 industrial policy – it has been reinforced as compared to the original version in the europe 2020 strategy, it does a lot more in terms of innovation, in terms of human capital, in terms of access to markets, access to finance – is certainly not exactly the same type of planned economy which i heard from some of the interventions. we definitely cannot establish a planned economy retroactively and give a capacity to the european commission to come with concrete intervention potential to cases when some of these companies, multinationals, decide to relocate. but i share your view when you say that these industrial policies should be better coordinated with the development of our financial institutions, including the european investment bank. indeed, not only the ford case but some other cases are controversial and we need to look into the decisions of the eib and see, especially in the context of the capital increase which the commission also has advocated in the case of the eib; without, of course, interfering too much with the business plan of the eib. but i think this would be justified if there is an effort in europe to increase the financial capacity of the eib. there should also be a discussion of how the eib could refocus its financing actually on the european member states and coordinate these projects better with the need to revive european manufacturing and in particular to help the relatively weaker regions. that also brings me to the questions of the mff because several speakers, including the rapporteur, miss harkin, but also mr daerden, mentioned the globalisation adjustment fund, which is indeed our key instrument for intervention in such cases. it is an ex post instrument, once restructuring has already been announced. but it is also an ex ante instrument because, through training, through various forms of education, promoting enterprise, it can actually prepare many workers involved for future economic or business activities. that is a capacity, a merit, a potential in the egf which i hope those in the european council will appreciate later this week when they consider the social dimension of the mff and the instruments that can support workers who are very often the victims of careless industrial decisions. i think this has been an extremely important discussion. i would like to connect it to the morning’s discussion when we were together here with vice-president tajani to discuss the questions of industry and what the commission can do for what the vice-president calls an industrial revolution in the european union, and i will certainly share with my colleagues the points and lessons of this debate, especially on the question of restructuring. i agree with many of you that we have a lot to do.
the debate is closed. written declarations (rule 149) the president of the court of justice shall be informed of the decision of the court of first instance.
the next item is the debate on the oral question to the commission on steel in the eu by amalia sartori, on behalf of the committee on industry, research and energy (o-000184/2012 - b7-0368/2012)
vice-president of the commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, it is clear that the speeches of all the members who have spoken this evening are aimed at the competitiveness of the european industrial system. the steel sector - as i said in my first speech, in reply to mr sartori's question - is a key element. of course, competing in the world today means facing a reality that is completely different from the past, as many members have pointed out during this debate. that is why, at the last competitiveness council, we discussed the subject of industrial policy, its strength at global level, the problems facing us in a context which has changed from that of the 1950s. the debate is also under way within the commission: i have received a letter on this general subject from seven highly industrialized countries - luxembourg, france, italy and germany - which insist that a debate be opened on this issue, as well as on the question of state aid. commissioner almunia, responsible for competition policy, intends to present a reform proposal. the debate is also open within the commission, because we cannot fail to keep up with the times, bearing in mind that the rules of competition are fundamental to growth and that we cannot fail to defend the internal market. but i understand from the contributions of all the members that this is a subject for discussion and reflection in the coming months if we want our industrial system to be competitive. the internal market policy - i am replying to mr pervenche berès - must also be involved in this action: commissioner barnier is certainly one of the strongest supporters of a european industrial policy and he has always given me support for all the initiatives i have wanted to take, including the steel initiative. this leads me to say that there has certainly been a political reversal in europe. i insist on the 20% target because, alongside the 20% co2 emission reduction and the 20% renewables, there is also the 20% of gross domestic product linked to industrial policy. of course, there is still much to be done in the steel sector: i am referring to the ets issue raised by several members of parliament. the commission has already given a positive reply on compensation: we have been able to authorize up to 85% of the amounts for state aid in this sector and we have included all the major industrial sectors, including steel. the commission has put forward a proposal on back-loading, and the ball is now in the court of the european parliament, which can give its decisive opinion, because without the support of parliament and the council nothing is possible in this area. but i have taken note of the concerns expressed, although we must do something - i believe it is possible - to reconcile environmental policy and industrial policy. i now reply to mr durant and i do so in french. the new framework programmes and horizon 2020 will offer new opportunities to finance research and development and to move towards more efficient production in terms of resources and energy. the steel industry, in conjunction with other industries, has already submitted a proposal for a new public-private partnership called spire. i think that these appropriations can therefore also be used in the next financial perspective. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's energy policy is not undermined by the fact that the community is not able to meet the demands of the world market. but a raw materials strategy is also essential for the steel sector. as you know, we have signed a number of agreements with non-european countries, and we have also launched the innovation partnership, particularly in the field of substitution, recycling and extraction of raw materials. this is a strong action, which will be financed under the current but also the next financial perspective and which will see the european parliament particularly involved. as far as terni is concerned, i believe that commissioner almunia has acted in full compliance with the rules in force. i think that the fact that the industrial site at terni has been kept compact and united is an absolutely positive element, which can allow its sale: unpacking would have been much more dangerous for the protection of that industrial site. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. as regards the pipeline, it is true that it was not integrated with the rest of the site; but it is a reality that is not - even geographically - closely linked, even though there is, as far as i understand, the willingness on the part of the present owner to sell the pipeline. i therefore believe that the problem raised by mr angelilli can also be solved by protecting an industrial site which is important not only for italy but for the whole of the european union. i therefore stress the importance of an industrial policy for the union and we will discuss this again at the next competitiveness council on 10 december, a competitiveness council to which we want to give greater importance and strengthen the role of industrial policy and the representatives of industry. i thank the cypriot presidency for its help in this matter. i also intend to convene the ministers of industry on the evening of 9 december to discuss the automobile crisis, which is closely linked to the steel crisis. the commission is strongly committed to protecting our industrial system, making it more modern, more competitive, more environmentally friendly, but also, of course, a fundamental instrument for overcoming the crisis and thus protecting employment. i am absolutely convinced that the sacrifices we have asked of our fellow citizens are absolutely indispensable. but when these sacrifices are not accompanied by a strong growth policy - and therefore also by a strong industrial policy - they risk not only being useless but, above all, being harmful to our fellow citizens. that is why it is important to work on both fronts and it is my responsibility and my will to support strongly the growth policy throughout the european union, by protecting, strengthening and making the steel industry more competitive.

the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done. for many years, as is well known, the european union has established an unprecedented network of repression, surveillance and prosecution mechanisms such as the euro-police, eurojust, frontex, anti-terrorism legislation, harmonisation of criminal legislation, external border surveillance system, strengthening of police and judicial cooperation. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european parliament is able to exercise its right to vote and to stand as a candidate in the elections to the european parliament. this increased file capacity is now being served by the new second generation schengen information system. the sys ii system also abolishes the personal data protection checks, since it allows personal data, including biometric data, to be recorded and any person who, in the arbitrary judgment of the law enforcement mechanisms, is suspected of having committed criminal offences to be subjected to discretionary surveillance. the amendments to the european parliament's report on data accuracy and the alleged supervision by the european data protection supervisor and the national supervisory authorities are intended to cover up the - in fact - orgy of the dossier at eu and national level. the european union's notorious area of freedom and security reveals its nightmarish form as a place of strangulation of basic human rights and fundamental freedoms and unchecked action of repressive mechanisms to protect the power of monopolies from the resistance of the peoples, from the struggles of the popular movement.

in writing. i voted in favour of the report on draft amending budget (dab) no 5/2012 for the mobilisation of the solidarity fund following the earthquakes in emilia-romagna, italy and amending the budget line for the preparatory action for the european year of volunteering 2011. it is regrettable that during the conciliation period the council sought to include this amending budget in the package of negotiations on the eu budget for the financial year 2013. in this way it caused an unjustified delay in the mobilisation of the solidarity fund related to the corrective budget and therefore in the more immediate relief of the inhabitants of the area affected by the earthquake. it is only thanks to the european parliament's position, which insisted that the mobilisation of the fund should not be held hostage to the outcome of the 2013 budget negotiations, that the council decided to approve ipa 5 - and this after the 2013 budget negotiations had reached a deadlock. this is a good example of the european parliament's responsible stance but raises the question of why the council insisted on including fp5 in the 2013 budget negotiations.
in writing. first of all, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on his report and welcome the mobilisation of the eu solidarity fund for the earthquakes in the emilia-romagna region of italy, which in may 2012 caused the death of 27 people, injured 350 and the evacuation of more than 45000 residents from the area. at the same time, significant damage was recorded to basic infrastructure, businesses, agriculture and cultural heritage. the priority now is to restore as soon as possible normal living conditions for the people affected by the earthquakes and to speed up the recovery of economic activity in the area. the eu solidarity fund is once again proving its importance in responding to major natural disasters by providing financial assistance to the affected states. however, i can only express my regret that it took six months to complete the procedure for mobilising the fund. the three institutions must therefore work to make the operations of the solidarity fund faster and more flexible. in this way we will be able to ensure the real added value of the instrument in question.
in writing. common agreement in the european parliament is the encouraging increase in citizens' activation through complaints of maladministration or breach of community law. this development clearly improves accountability, control and therefore democratic functioning at both union and member state level. however, the delayed or incorrect transposition of community law in the member states is still a phenomenon. this own-initiative report, which i voted in favour of, calls on the commission to use all possible means to ensure that the member states transpose union legislation in a timely and correct manner and reiterates the importance of strengthening and coordinating the legal training of the member states' judges in order to create a european awareness of community law.
in writing. i voted in favour of mrs javella's report because i believe it is a balanced report which sets out policy on the future of shale gas in the union and at the same time gives due attention to the environmental dimension of the issue. shale gas, as a new form of energy at a critical time for the european economy, can be a source of growth and a breath of fresh air for european industries in dealing with the extremely high energy costs and energy dependence of the european union.
in writing. it remains worrying that most of the reports made by european citizens concern issues of fundamental rights and justice. the number of references in this direction increased in 2011, mainly due to the incorporation of the charter of fundamental rights into the treaty, and concern a matter which the committee responsible has examined repeatedly. in this report, which i voted in favour of, the european parliament calls on the commission to apply the charter not conservatively but dynamically, responding to the expectations of citizens as they arise from the incorporation of the charter into the treaty. at the same time, and despite the fact that its position was not accepted at the first stage, the european parliament insists that the abolition of the age limit for referrals should be adopted and that open hearings in the european parliament should be introduced as symbolic and substantive acts to strengthen democracy and accountability at community level.
in writing. – i voted in favour of the report. the main focus of the committee on petitions’ work during 2011 has been on the environment and fundamental rights/justice. this is no different from other years and therefore only confirms the trends. the prominence of these issues during 2011 runs like a beacon through all the work of the committee: discussions on petitions in committee, new petitions received, reports and hearings and fact-finding visits. as expressed in the report, the need to step up efforts to provide better information to direct citizens’ concerns to the right interlocutor and to explain the competencies of different levels of government and public administration is the goal.
mr president, parliament has full faith in the commissioner. we know that she is a woman who not only tries to do her best but someone who does the best possible. the target, as we see it, is not a success in doha. the target is a success in the negotiations for an agreement in 2015. this is a country-by-country fight. we are fighting to win the hearts and minds of people and bureaucracies, each time in one more country and then in another and another. qatar has been one of the difficult partners in this negotiation. now it holds in its hands the very future of these negotiations for an agreement in 2015 – for one whole year. may it be that, by the end of cop 18, qatar becomes a pioneer in these negotiations. it has, as you also told us yesterday, the highest per capita income in the world. may it be a main and important contributor for climate action across the world. nevertheless, the message that comes from the parliament is that you are not going to doha empty-handed. a year ago we had the decision in durban on lulucf. as you might know, we, with the council, are way ahead in negotiations for a decision on lulucf legislation. this is a clear commitment from our side that we believe in these negotiations and that we are willing to do everything to make them a reality.
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. - the mr president, first of all i would like to thank all my colleagues, commissioner füle and all those who have contributed to the preparation of this strategic report of the european parliament. the report comes at a critical juncture. · at a time when the economic crisis is shaking the european fabric in many countries, both inside and outside the european union, there is talk of enlargement fatigue. the main objective of this report is therefore to emphasize the importance of enlargement policy. a policy which has proved to be the most successful expression of the union's external action and the quintessence of its soft power. for decades, enlargement has indeed helped to consolidate peace in europe. it was a key factor in the reunification of the continent after the end of the cold war and continues to provide incentives for further reforms and democratisation in the countries looking to join. the european union has made a major contribution to the enlargement process, and it is important that the union's efforts to achieve this are recognised. the disappointment some feel is nevertheless justified. the union seems to be setting the bar higher and higher, while statements by european politicians have at times damaged its credibility, especially in terms of the capacity of a particular country to join or absorb. the report confirms the central role of the copenhagen criteria and attaches great importance to the policy of conditionality. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the accession of the candidate countries to the european union is not delayed. the union's demands on each country must be absolutely clear from the outset. at the same time, all the concrete steps must be defined from the outset with benchmarks - time-dials - which will allow an objective assessment of a country's progress. in the context of the current economic crisis, which is creating legitimate fears and uncertainty about the future, there is a need for better information and promotion of the benefits of the enlargement process. this is important for the citizens of the european union and for the citizens of the candidate countries. the main issue, however, is the credibility of the union: the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to promote the integration of the candidate countries into the european union. the credibility of the union's promise of accession is an essential component of the policy of selectivity. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the application of the principle of subsidiarity to the member states' taxation systems is a step in the right direction. i would like to take this opportunity to welcome the commission's new negotiating approach which puts the rule of law and fundamental rights at the heart of the accession process. i look forward to the debate and thank once again all those who have helped to draw up this report.
on behalf of the efd group. mr president, first of all i would like to congratulate mrs coppa on her excellent report. the report confirms the european parliament's commitment to enlargement policy and introduces innovative approaches to improve it. the new realities, such as the economic crisis and global challenges, call for a new approach to enlargement. the commission has already made a number of interesting proposals in this respect. the recommendation in the report that bilateral disputes should be settled before accession negotiations are opened is particularly welcome. i also welcome the proposal to introduce performance and benchmarks into the accession process, thus making it a more transparent and democratic process, and the proposal to strengthen the social dimension of government by enhancing the involvement of civil society. potential enlargement, provided that the copenhagen criteria are respected and the european acquis and its values are upheld, is a tool for strengthening the union. the bible says: "be fruitful and become many and fill the earth". i personally hope that the apparent present disintegration of the european union will be temporary.
mr president, it is unnecessary for me to add my own comments to those of my colleagues on the very good work and the high quality of mrs coppa's report. i shall only refer to the crucial issue of the outstanding bilateral issues which should be resolved by the candidate countries. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very important one. the report, and in particular paragraph 26, addresses this issue in a realistic, politically sound and clear way. the outstanding bilateral problems, in particular those relating to good neighbourly relations between a candidate country and a member state, must be resolved before accession negotiations can begin. after all that, there's one more thing left to add: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is a serious matter which i think we should look at again.
mr president, in paragraphs 26, 27 and 28 the report attempts to turn international disputes, including those concerning military occupation such as kosovo and cyprus, into bilateral disputes under the auspices of the european union, which will undertake to resolve them on the basis of the overall interests of the union, as expressly stated, and not on the basis of international law. the report calls on the european union to intervene in the political system of the acceding countries by financing, through pre-accession aid, the mechanisms of the bourgeois state, various ngos and the labour aristocracy of the trade unions. the report, however, expresses the increasingly aggressive, imperialist character and role of the european union and calls for an intensification of this role against the peoples and member states and the acceding countries as well as the peoples of europe in particular. the european union is not a party to the treaty of rome, but it is a party to the treaty of rome.
member of the commission. − mr president, this has been an important debate with important messages. what a perfect time to remind ourselves, our member states, the commission and our aspirant candidate countries, firstly, that enlargement has never been a part of the problem! it has always been a part of the solution; secondly, that the european union has never been about building walls, but about eliminating dividing lines through its values and principles; and thirdly, that there is no more powerful instrument of transformation than enlargement. this is an absolutely unique instrument we have in our hands. we need to use it wisely throughout our neighbourhood. that leads me to the important contributions in this debate. i see six of them. the first one is the contribution to the debate of the interaction of the integration process, going deeper and deeper and, at the same time, extending the european union. what has made this european union big and strong? enlargement alone? i doubt it. going deeper and deeper? i doubt it. it is a combination of the two. let us not cut off one our arms now because we are facing many problems and say that the time for the enlargement will come. no! it is this interaction between enlargement and deeper integration which is actually making the european union a stronger union. it is in that context, whether we call it an association membership or whatever, that we should have that discussion about a multilayered european union. whether we call it political union or a federation of national states, it needs to be an enlargement-friendly union. at a time when the member states are debating the various levels of the integration, let us open at some point the issue of to what extent we allow candidate countries – maybe not those which are in the pipeline, but others, to which some of you referred – different ways of being a part of this tremendous european project. the second message concerns credibility – credibility through the lessons learned and credibility through engagement. we are being creative and we will continue to be creative because it is through that engagement that we are fighting reform fatigue. i am not too concerned about enlargement fatigue. we need to be aware that, if we are not careful enough, enlargement fatigue could turn into reform fatigue and then i would say that we have a problem. any problem in our neighbourhood is our problem. a third point concerns economic governance. let us make sure that aspirant and candidate countries are part of our efforts to address the issues of economic governance. let us make sure that through the accession negotiations we are not addressing only the acquis of the past, but also the acquis of the future. a fourth point concerns adequate financing. as far as i am concerned, it is not necessarily about more money. it is about those resources being spent better and this is definitely one of the issues where i need your support. the fifth point concerns bilateral issues. enlargement is not about importing open bilateral issues into the european union. we need to make sure that it does not also blow up the accession negotiation. we need to be sure that the unresolved, unaddressed bilateral issues do not create mines which could blow up before our eyes in the framework of the accession process. i am very open to finding ways – including the one of arbitration, turning to the hague and so on – to address those bilateral issues in parallel to the accession negotiation process. let us discuss the deadline by which you would like to see all the bilateral issues solved. i would be in favour of that deadline being the signing of the accession treaty because it is that accession process which creates a strong momentum for addressing those issues. my last point: let me finish where i started, with the nobel peace prize. if i think about the nobel peace prize – maybe it is my interpretation, maybe i am wrong – i think this european union got that prize exactly for its ability to put the deepening of integration and enlargement at the service of its people. needless to say, i see this nobel peace prize as a strong signal to the european union at this critical time; a signal not to look over our shoulders, not to look back, but to look forward.
i have to say that i am not in favour of this. mr president, three final points i would like to make after this very interesting debate. firstly, the need to pay attention to the social dimension of enlargement, a subject which has been ignored for many years. we hope that the commission will put emphasis on social issues in the context of the evaluation of the negotiating chapter 19. it must ensure the participation of trade unions and civil society in the screening and monitoring processes and their targeted support through the pre-accession assistance mechanism. secondly, a subject which has been discussed a great deal here, namely bilateral relations. the bilateral issues, the bilateral differences are a reality. i therefore agree with the commissioner that they must be resolved as soon as possible. the union cannot afford to introduce open questions and there must be mechanisms - and we can all be very creative in that - to help in this direction. the third point is the budgetary part. the union must have the appropriate financial resources to match its growing ambitions. i therefore find the substantial reduction of resources of around 9% in chapter 4 of the next financial framework unacceptable. in conclusion, mr vigenin and i, the ipa-ii rapporteur, stressed the need to simplify the procedures for pre-accession assistance and i thank the commissioner in particular for his response to this central issue. i would like to join my colleagues in congratulating commissioner füle on his tireless efforts to push forward the great enlargement project. we are at a critical juncture, and our decisions and our next steps will determine the entire future of this project.
president-in-office of the council, on behalf of the vice-president of the commission/high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy. − mr president, i am honoured to speak to you today in accordance with the treaty on the common and security and defence policy on behalf of the high representative. the four parliamentary reports we are discussing are timely and relevant; they reflect the world we live in, where an action in a far corner of the world could have an impact in our home town. they demonstrate that our security is interconnected in various ways. hence i would like to express my appreciation for the amount of work which lies behind these four reports and for their quality. your suggestions and recommendations are important to us and we are happy to have parliament on our side as our partner but also as a fair critic. with three new missions on the ground in the sahel, south sudan and horn of africa and with two more under preparation, for mali and libya, we have seen some dynamic developments over the past year. we have also seen clear results from our efforts. the number of pirate attacks off the horn of africa has shrunk significantly. there have only been five successful attacks this year compared to 25 last year and 47 in 2010. security inside somalia has also improved and the government has now extended its power over a great part of its territory previously ruled by al-shabaab. eutm somalia played an important role in this positive development as 3000 somali soldiers will be trained by the end of this year and they are taking up their responsibility for somalia’s security. but despite these tangible results we must not become complacent. the high representative has said this on numerous occasions and it is worth repeating: we must continue to resource our missions and operations properly and we must continue working towards the goals we have set out for our ongoing missions and our operations. the people that we send into the field are doing a tremendous job often in very dangerous and difficult environments. we are very grateful for their work and for their dedication. our missions are effective and their results are sustainable only if they are part of a comprehensive approach. the example of the horn of africa is a good illustration of our approach. in the horn, we employ policies and instruments in a consistent and mutually reinforcing manner. in addition to the three csp operations and on the basis of a jointly agreed strategic framework for the region, there is strong political engagement, support for amisom through the african peace facility, development cooperation and humanitarian aid. we also work very closely with a variety of partners, which increases our impact and also leads to a better use of our resources. the comprehensive approach is about defining a correct policy mix and the proper sequencing of instruments, both at eu level and with member states. it is about overall consistency in our external relations, about bringing security to populations affected by conflict and alleviating poverty. as we are stepping up operational engagement in new crisis areas such as the sahel, we must continue working in a comprehensive manner. let me now turn to capabilities. the csdp allows the european union to act, but to act we must have capabilities. member states provide these capabilities and we all know that the financial crisis is putting a heavy burden on national budgets. defence capabilities are particularly affected. this is why our work on pooling and sharing is so important. synergies with wider european policies, research and development, dual use and the defence industry are also crucial. these themes are prominent in the report by mr danjean. both topics were on the agenda of the council of ministers of defence on 19 november 2012 and member states are taking a great interest. you are all aware that the president of the european council has decided to put defence on the agenda of the european council at the end of december 2013. this offers a very good opportunity to address these challenges at the highest political level. there are some common themes in the four reports: the need for comprehensiveness, close coordination in-house, but also with partners, full use of the post-lisbon set up, better use of resources and the efficiency of our efforts. indeed, in these trying economic times, these cannot be stressed enough. cyber security is a case in point. the report by mr kelam sets out the imminent need for stepping up eu-wide preventive measures and to improve horizontal cooperation within the eu institutions and agencies. we also need to bolster public-private partnerships in cyber security. the european external action service and the commission are preparing a joint commission-eeas communication on eu cyber security to ensure the adequate level of cyber security preparedness, also looking at cyber defence capabilities and training, as well as a coherent international cyber policy for the european union. we have already started active international cyber security cooperation, with an emphasis on the united states and on emerging markets as well as nato, the united nations, oecd, osce and other international organisations. they need to enhance collective knowledge of climate-related security challenges, as set out in the report by mr tarand. indeed, it is necessary to pursue efforts at eu and member state level, as well as in dialogue with external partners and civil society to identify climate security needs and to further strengthen our understanding of interlinkages between climate change development, environmental degradation, natural resources, migration or conflict. the challenge is to enhance early warning and move to early preventive action. the european union and member states are leading international efforts to enhance climate security by initiating policy dialogue, including at the un security council level and in its bilateral relations and by supporting concrete initiatives in the fields of climate risk management and adaptation. one example is the global climate change alliance, which has a strong focus on climate adaptation in the most climate vulnerable regions of the world. another area of work relates to the solidarity clause, which is designed for situations of major emergencies affecting the member states. the european external action service and the commission are currently working on a joint proposal to implement the clause. the report presented by mr paşcu is again very timely. we concur with the main lines of the report. i would like to underline some points which seem particularly important to us. as far as structures and procedures are concerned, the eu needs to possess crisis response structures with 24/7 monitoring and response capacity, able to provide early warning and up-to-date situation awareness to all relevant actors on all types of hazards and crises or disasters. the european union situation room and the commission’s monitoring and information centre are ready to support the process. also revised eu emergency and crisis coordination arrangements are suitable in this context. responding at eu political level in a coherent, efficient and timely way to crises of such a scale and nature requires one single set of arrangements. the four reports at hand today, on a variety of topics, all relate to european security, both internal and external. we look forward to continuing our work with you in our joint efforts to protect the security of our citizens and make the european union an increasingly effective contributor to global peace and security. i am really looking forward to the forthcoming debate and to your comments.
i am speaking on behalf of the ppe group. mr president, it is important that today, in a time of crisis, the european parliament sends a strong message of european solidarity and demonstrates its will and conviction that the solution to today's problems can only be achieved through policies with a genuinely pan-european dimension. the creation of a comprehensive common security and defence policy in europe has been a vision for decades in the history of european integration. the introduction into the treaty of lisbon of the two innovative clauses proposed by the european convention on the future of europe is moving in this direction. the final text of mr paşcu's report, which i congratulate on its work and warmly thank for the cooperation we have had throughout this period, presents in a realistic but ambitious way specific requirements and guidelines on the measures to implement the mutual defence and solidarity clause. in this process, clearer and different views were expressed on the character and nature of the two innovative terms. however, the complaints about the increased cost of the whole project, the possible problems and the question of the competences falling within nato's competence express more the fears of some member states than the reality. the truth is that the gradual establishment of a genuine common defence, security and defence policy could drastically reduce the unbearable cost of european defence spending without in the least affecting the commitments made in the framework of nato. mr president, the vice-president of the commission/high representative must now submit before the end of 2012 the joint proposal for a council decision setting out the arrangements for the two clauses. parliament is therefore awaiting this proposal.
the s&d group is calling for a new approach to the problem of the environment. mr president, i would like to congratulate mr danjean and all the other members on their reports, which are an excellent example of the creative work being done within the european parliament's subcommittee on defence and security. the socialists and democrats wish to stress the importance of strengthening european identity also in matters of defence and security. the union must have the means and capabilities necessary to develop its strategic autonomy and its capacity to provide security for its citizens. in the context of the current economic crisis, wider synergies and joint actions at european level are both imperative and functionally useful. but they should also allow the release of resources at national level which can be used in critical social areas. the european union must be able to provide effective defence and security for the citizens of europe, and that is the great stake.

the s&d group is calling for a new approach to the problem of the environment. mr president, i thank the commissioner very much for coming to the european parliament and for her statement today. it is obvious to me, and we all agree, that the issue of protecting the personal data of european citizens is of the utmost importance and requires the necessary seriousness and attention from all of us. i would like to stress once again that we in the european parliament attach particular importance to this subject. the european parliament is therefore, and will continue to be, particularly strict and demanding of the other institutions of the union in this respect. as you know, commissioner, the european parliament has always wanted to have a single legislative instrument, a regulation covering all aspects and all areas of personal data protection, so that the public and private sectors are treated equally and that the processing of personal data by judicial and police authorities is also included. unfortunately, this wish was not respected, and today we have to deal with the proposals for a regulation and a directive. the commission's proposals were welcomed by parliament, but we decided to consider and treat them as a single legislative package, both in practice and in substance. i would like to stress that in our view there can be no regulation without a directive. we will insist on the logic of the package in order to ensure consistency and legal certainty. i am glad, commissioner, that your speech today has confirmed once again that the commission sees and treats a regulation and a directive as one package. i want you to believe that we have no complaints from the commission. the commission's proposal is to be submitted to the council for approval, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept it. unfortunately, i cannot say the same for the council. i wonder why the council is absent from today's debate. why is the council not here today to answer the oral question we have put? let me say that this is not only a sign of disrespect for the institution of the european parliament but also, as i feel compelled to conclude, it suggests that the council is not giving the necessary importance to this matter which is so important to european citizens. the council is unfortunately delaying its work on the directive. i wonder - and i think it is reasonable - why the council is refusing to cooperate, as it shows by its attitude. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work is a very good one. the aim is to complete the procedures and implement the regulation and the directive as a package, to provide european citizens with a high level of protection of their personal data.
member of the commission. − mr president, i would like to thank all the speakers for their support of the commission’s proposals. i would like to refer again to two main points. firstly, i would like to reconfirm that, for the commission, progress on this very important issue means that we are going to negotiate both the regulation and the directive as a package. we really mean it. we have gone through two pieces of legislation, in spite of parliament’s request, but now we have to focus on the procedure. i would like to confirm to everybody that we are going to insist on that. we are going to negotiate both pieces of legislation as a package. the second point i would like to stress is that we are going to do our best in order to ensure that the new legislation – the new post-lisbon legislation on data protection – will be taken into account in terms of all the agreements and proposals that have some relevance to this data protection issue. i think that everybody here understands that this is a major issue, because it refers to human rights; it refers to the basic principles set out in the treaties which, in one way or another, have to guide our decisions. i know that an impasse has been reached. we would like to overcome it. i would like to say that the commission is here and that it is really very willing to cooperate in order to overcome the difficulties both with the council and with parliament. of course, we will welcome any initiatives by the irish presidency if it, on behalf of the council, of course, would like to overcome the impasse.
